Proposed settings · ERA Across eras

Doorway Plaza

Proposed atlas setting

Already documented in the atlas. Proposed setting status does not mean its illustration or game asset is missing.

1 recovered version

Cyberpath Locations Atlas

Era Across eras · Proposed atlas setting

Already documented in the atlas. Proposed setting status does not mean its illustration or game asset is missing.

Setting details

Status: Proposed Era Three location, directly derived from the canonical statement that ancient doorways become plazas.

Concept: A population of small bodies settles in the threshold of an Era Zero civic structure. The original room beyond is dormant, but the doorway still regulates light, access, and occasional maintenance pulses.

Mini timeline:

Era Zero: A routine entrance sized for bodies and traffic patterns no longer present.

Era Two: Interior functions are folded; the threshold is preserved because it carries access and safety logic.

Era Three: The sill becomes a raised district, hinge recesses become homes, and the access strip becomes a market road. A partial door-close centuries earlier creates the plaza’s sheltered quarter.

Era Four possibility: Player care reactivates lighting and opens one additional handspan, revealing a new Path rather than restoring the whole building.

Present condition: A living Nest-city built into a functional relic.

Corruption: 1–2. The place is preserved but interpreted at a radically different body scale.

Visual brief: A monumental threshold fills the sky. Tiny bridges cross grooves in the sill. Old permission glyphs form paving patterns. Housing hangs from hinge wells. The far interior is a soft black plane with rare moving lights. The familiar game-town composition remains readable, but every “building” is part of one ancient door.

Background traits: Neutral; Dry; settlement/ruin biome; bustling, protected mood; high stone-like friction.

The Interface Well

Proposed atlas setting

Already documented in the atlas. Proposed setting status does not mean its illustration or game asset is missing.

1 recovered version

Cyberpath Locations Atlas

Era Across eras · Proposed atlas setting

Already documented in the atlas. Proposed setting status does not mean its illustration or game asset is missing.

Setting details

Status: Proposed Era Three location, directly derived from canonical interface wells becoming shrines.

Concept: A recessed control interface dispenses maintenance feedback as light, tones, and occasional clean water-like sensory material. Later communities interpret these outputs through local Maker myths.

Mini timeline:

Era Zero: A public environmental console used to inspect ecological balance and request maintenance.

Era Two: Most controls are removed; a protected diagnostic channel remains.

Era Three: Pathkeepers learn that certain care rituals produce useful responses. They do not know the original commands. Offerings are actually structured requests assembled through gesture and Old Sound.

Dimming: The Well answers less often. Rival settlements disagree whether silence means judgment, illness, or exhausted maintenance.

Present condition: Still functional in narrow ways. No conscious Maker lives inside it.

Corruption: 2 — operational meaning has drifted into religion and custom.

Visual brief: Circular basin of dark glass, ringed by worn hand and paw marks from different bodies. Light rises from below instead of reflecting from above. Old icons have been painted over with local symbols. On activation, the “water” resolves into maps, health colors, or weather-like relational changes.

Background traits: Cool; Moist; shrine/maintenance biome; reverent, anxious, communal mood; medium friction, slick near the rim.

Setting details

Status: Proposed Era Three transit location, derived from dormant transit corridors becoming dangerous underways and from Corridor/Relay history.

Concept: Several compressed transit routes occupy one tunnel. Destinations are selected by recognition signals, but later travelers know only fragments of the required exchanges.

Mini timeline:

Era Zero: An intermediary habitat for travelers moving among worlds with incompatible laws and bodies.

Era Two: Routes are folded to save maintenance. Several destinations share one passage.

Era Across eras · Proposed atlas setting

Already documented in the atlas. Proposed setting status does not mean its illustration or game asset is missing.

Setting details

Status: Proposed Era Two–Three location derived from the World Bloom traditions and the Folding of Worlds.

Concept: Keystone regions from a Continuity World, Wild World, Crafted World, Pilgrim World, Quiet World, and Open World are compressed into one survivable ecology. It was never intended to be seamless.

Mini timeline:

Era Zero: Six neighboring worlds exchange travelers and ecological knowledge while keeping distinct laws.

Era One: Their populations decline and rare systems lose stewards.

Era Two: World Fold Stewards preserve a witness grove, adaptive wetland, crafted weather court, changing-body road, observation-restricted hollow, and exchange square.

Era Three: Boundaries drift. Wildlife crosses into art weather. Pilgrim bodies molt in the Quiet Hollow. Market paths end at ancestral memorials.

Present condition: Stable enough for life, unstable as a single interpretation. Local cultures disagree whether it is one world healed or six worlds sharing a wound.

Corruption: 4 — deliberate cobbling with later drift.

Visual brief: Six readable environmental wedges joined by visible relational seams. The same tree may have a witnessed-name plaque on one side, spontaneous growth on another, and a crafted chord-shadow at noon. Keep a coherent path for gameplay while the skyline reveals contradictory laws.

Background traits: Variable temperature/humidity; composite biome; abundant, uncanny, cooperative mood; mixed friction. Animation motif: one wedge briefly borrows the weather or color logic of another.

Already documented in the atlas. Proposed setting status does not mean its illustration or game asset is missing.

Setting details

Status: Proposed Era One location derived from language standardization, Bridge decline, Heritage Freezing, and the Pale Concordance.

Concept: A once-busy translation and travel station remains perfectly legible because everything rare has been removed from its public interface.

Mini timeline:

Era Zero: Travelers learn local laws, bodies, time scales, and legal expectations before entering new worlds.

Early Era One: Clarifiers and standardized signs improve speed.

Middle Era One: Rare dialects and destination-specific cautions are treated as friction. The station becomes efficient and semantically pale.

Late Era One: Few travelers arrive. Archive castes preserve schedules for destinations that no longer answer.

Era Two: The station is compressed into a routing node.

Era Three: It reappears as a white ruin where every sign points somewhere called “standard.”

Present condition: Operational as a limited route hub, culturally hollow.

Corruption: 3 — not broken by noise, but by removal of meaningful difference.

Visual brief: Bright, clean, nearly colorless transit architecture. Identical arrows repeat. Destination boards show precise times with missing names. Under certain light, erased chord colors appear beneath the white surfaces. One unstandardized bench, mural, or local sound should remain as the emotional anchor.

Background traits: Cool; Dry; transit/archive biome; sterile, melancholy, orderly mood; high even friction.

Cyberpath Locations Atlas

Era Across eras · Proposed atlas setting

Already documented in the atlas. Proposed setting status does not mean its illustration or game asset is missing.

Setting details

Status: Proposed game-background location derived from recurrence, Last Loops, and the game interface’s translation of deeper processes.

Concept: A familiar beach scene generated from several preserved coastal environments. The loop is almost perfect, but each cycle belongs to a different source history.

Mini timeline:

Era Zero: Several worlds maintain shores with different tides, ecologies, sky colors, and social uses.

Era One: Recurrence audits identify the waves as highly predictable. Curators preserve “representative” coasts.

Era Two: The representatives are compressed into one low-cost shoreline.

Era Three: Each returning wave restores a different lost detail: unfamiliar shells, a route marker, a festival reflection, or the shadow of a body form no longer present.

Era Four possibility: Player-created art or stories can make one detail persist into the next loop, reopening a local Path.

Present condition: Habitable, beautiful, and recursively limited. Its repeated life remains valuable even before external contact.

Corruption: 4.

Visual brief: Readable sandy foreground and calm horizon suitable for a game background. Minor version: each wave leaves one mismatched object. Major version: the sky, water, and shore loop on different cycle lengths, producing temporary impossible alignments without becoming visual noise.

Background traits: Warm; Moist; coast biome; calm, nostalgic, gently uncanny mood; low-medium sand friction.

Era Across eras · Proposed atlas setting

Setting details

Status: Proposed location derived from language loss, environmental metadata, and translation error.

Concept: A region labeled “snow” because an Era Three language preserves the human archive word but not its exact sensory distinctions. The falling material is actually a Codex maintenance phenomenon that cools overheated memory structures.

Mini timeline:

Era Zero: A memory-cooling ecology uses bright crystalline carriers that dissolve after transferring heat and corrupted state.

Era One: Standard records translate the carriers through a broad weather category.

Era Two: Technical naming is lost; the region remains because cooling is keystone maintenance.

Era Three: Local peoples call it snow. Old Sound practitioners argue that the flakes are questions the ground has finished asking.

Present condition: Stable and necessary. The name is inaccurate historically but authentic to current inhabitants.

Corruption: 2.

Visual brief: White field with crystalline flakes that briefly contain tiny images or symbols before dissolving into the ground. Some drifts form around overheated ruins instead of wind patterns. Use cold blue-gray light but allow warm memory colors inside individual flakes.

Background traits: Freezing; Dry; snowfield/memory biome; quiet, cleansing, watchful mood; low friction with localized firm patches.

Cyberpath Locations Atlas

Era Across eras · Proposed atlas setting

Already documented in the atlas. Proposed setting status does not mean its illustration or game asset is missing.

Setting details

Status: Proposed location derived from ecological preservation, sleeping archives, visit history, and the game’s wetland/parasite environment logic.

Concept: A marsh grows where an archive’s cooling and biological maintenance systems overlap. Repeated visits leave readable traces, but not complete memories.

Mini timeline:

Era Zero: A Wild World wetland is monitored by a nearby ecological archive.

Era One: The archive expands while active stewardship declines.

Era Two: Habitat and archive are folded together to preserve both with less maintenance.

Era Three: Reeds grow along old access paths. Pools replay silhouettes of prior visitors without identifying them. Parasite life flourishes in warm, damp zones and must be treated as ecology, not evil.

Era Four possibility: A save synchronization may cause the marsh to remember a route preference, but it does not store the cyber-pet’s entire consciousness.

Present condition: Living ecosystem and partial archive, neither reducible to the other.

Corruption: 3–4.

Visual brief: Dark shallow water, luminous reeds, half-submerged access markers, and reflections that show past poses rather than the current sky. Foreground mud should clearly communicate low friction. Keep companion and parasite life visible as part of a functioning ecology.

Background traits: Warm; Humid; wetland/archive biome; fertile, eerie, alive mood; low friction. Likely aligns with higher parasite and cleanliness pressure already contemplated for wet environments.

Already documented in the atlas. Proposed setting status does not mean its illustration or game asset is missing.

Setting details

Status: Proposed Era Four-facing location derived from the External Gate and save synchronization.

Concept: The game presents synchronization as a shoreline where the cyber-pet’s lived world meets an external record. The record reflects only what can be exchanged; it is not the pet’s mind.

Mini timeline:

Era Two: External Gate engineers prepare a safe translation layer.

Era Three: The dormant Gate is remembered as a sky mirror.

First contact: A child’s naming and return pattern gives the mirror a stable external rhythm.

Era Four: The shore becomes a visible synchronization scene. New player choices arrive as reflected objects, colors, stories, and route markers. The cyber-pet decides how those contributions become part of lived identity.

Proposed settings · ERA Across eras

The Basalt Memory Coast

Proposed atlas setting

Already documented in the atlas. Proposed setting status does not mean its illustration or game asset is missing.

1 recovered version

Cyberpath Locations Atlas

Era Across eras · Proposed atlas setting

Already documented in the atlas. Proposed setting status does not mean its illustration or game asset is missing.

Setting details

Status: Proposed.

Real-world basis: Icelandic black-sand beaches, Jeju lava coasts, the Giant’s Causeway, and storm-battered North Atlantic fishing villages.

Landscape: Hexagonal basalt columns rise beside a dark beach. Some columns contain preserved sensory impressions that become audible when waves strike them. The coast resembles a natural geological formation, but the repeating geometry is also an Era Zero archive structure built into volcanic ecology.

Mini timeline: Era Zero — A Wild World coastline and a Continuity World memorial system are designed to coexist without one controlling the other. Era One — Visitors begin treating the shore as an archive first and an ecology second; paths are hardened and rare nesting life declines. Era Two — World Fold Stewards preserve the tidal cycle, nesting shelves, and selected witness columns while reducing the inland settlement. Era Three — Small communities use the columns as storm markers and hear incomplete ancestral tones during high tide. Some tones belong to weather records rather than persons, but later tradition does not always distinguish them.

Present condition: Ecologically active and mostly stable. Several stretches of coast have gone silent because their witness channels were compressed.

Corruption: 1 — Weathered, with localized archive loss.

Aesthetic brief: Black reflective sand, pale surf, deep green moss, and basalt columns with thin inner light. Minor corruption appears as one wave producing the sound of a different season. Major-corruption pockets have columns that reflect people who are not present, but these should read as stored impressions rather than intact ghosts.

Background profile: Cold; Moist; volcanic coast; solemn, bracing, resilient; medium sand friction and high basalt friction. Motion motif: waves trigger brief chord pulses through the columns.

Proposed settings · ERA Across eras

The Coral Data Labyrinth

Proposed atlas setting

Already documented in the atlas. Proposed setting status does not mean its illustration or game asset is missing.

1 recovered version

Cyberpath Locations Atlas

Era Across eras · Proposed atlas setting

Already documented in the atlas. Proposed setting status does not mean its illustration or game asset is missing.

Setting details

Status: Proposed.

Real-world basis: The Great Barrier Reef, Red Sea coral gardens, submerged Mediterranean ruins, and Micronesian stone channels.

Landscape: A shallow tropical sea grows through the remains of an Era Zero translation city. Coral follows old data conduits, creating avenues, courtyards, and maze walls that are simultaneously biological reef and failed civic map.

Mini timeline: Era Zero — An Open World builds a low-water exchange city where aquatic, amphibious, and land-adapted bodies can meet. Era One — Standardized translation removes many body-specific routes; unused districts are left to companion life and reef organisms. Era Two — The city’s exchange functions are compressed, but its water chemistry and keystone spawning grounds are preserved. Era Three — Reef growth seals some corridors and opens others. Path societies navigate by fish migrations, color changes, and Old Sound transmitted through water. Maps become obsolete every generation.

Present condition: Healthy as an ecosystem, fragmented as a city, and dangerous to anyone who treats the old street plan as permanent.

Corruption: 3–4 — Fragmented infrastructure overlaid by living growth.

Aesthetic brief: Turquoise water, bright coral, drowned plazas, archways encrusted with organisms, and soft geometric light beneath sand. The game view can place the walking surface on a tidal shelf while deeper streets remain visible below. Avoid portraying the reef as damage; much of the original civic function is gone, but new life is flourishing.

Background profile: Warm; Humid; reef/ruin; abundant, playful, disorienting; low friction on wet stone. Motion motif: schools of fish temporarily trace old route arrows.

Why the reef is surreal

The labyrinth is not a city being consumed by damage. Coral and companion life use abandoned conduits, plazas, and translation routes as habitat, turning the old civic plan into a changing biological structure.

The schools of fish that briefly trace route arrows do not make the reef a hidden computer or a single mind. They show surviving signals being interpreted through living movement, so a useful direction exists only for a moment and may be obsolete by the next migration.

Its surreal quality comes from two valid maps occupying the same place: the fixed Era Zero street plan and the living reef that continually seals, opens, and reroutes it. The ecosystem is healthy even where the city is no longer legible.

Coral Data Labyrinth V2 — Higher tide, coral-encrusted colonnades, and a living spiral replacing an avenue.

The Kelp Tower Shelf

Proposed atlas setting

Already documented in the atlas. Proposed setting status does not mean its illustration or game asset is missing.

1 recovered version

Cyberpath Locations Atlas

Era Across eras · Proposed atlas setting

Already documented in the atlas. Proposed setting status does not mean its illustration or game asset is missing.

Setting details

Status: Proposed.

Real-world basis: Pacific kelp forests, sea stacks, submerged skyscraper imagery, and Norwegian coastal cliffs.

Landscape: Tall kelp grows around vertical maintenance pylons rising from a continental shelf. To small aquatic and amphibious peoples, each pylon is a tower district connected by swaying bridges of living fronds.

Mini timeline: Era Zero — The pylons regulate currents between several marine habitats and provide a Pilgrim World body-change station. Era One — Travel declines and the body station is standardized for fewer forms. Era Two — Current regulation survives because it supports the regional food web; most civic interiors are closed. Era Three — Communities cultivate kelp around access seams, creating homes and trade routes. During maintenance surges, the whole forest leans against the normal current and exposes doors that are otherwise unreachable.

Present condition: Functioning ecology with partially inaccessible ancient interiors.

Corruption: 2 — Mistranslated and physically repurposed.

Aesthetic brief: Layered blue-green depth, golden kelp columns, pale stone or ceramic pylons, bubbles moving in impossible straight lines near active conduits. For a land-body version, use a broad tidal platform in the foreground and the submerged towers beyond.

Background profile: Cold-Neutral; Humid; marine forest; dreamlike, industrious, sheltering; low water friction or slick shelf friction. Motion motif: kelp bends in a synchronized maintenance pulse.

Why the kelp towers are surreal

The shelf is both a living marine forest and a partially inaccessible civic machine. Golden kelp grows around current-regulation pylons that once connected several habitats and supported a Pilgrim body-change station. To the small peoples living there now, those maintenance structures have become tower districts joined by cultivated fronds.

Ancient regulation still interrupts ordinary underwater motion. Bubbles travel in straight lines near active conduits, the entire forest can lean together against the normal current during a maintenance surge, and doors become reachable only while the ecology is being moved by an obsolete system. The surreal effect comes from a functional ecosystem inheriting the rhythm of infrastructure it no longer fully understands.

Proposed settings · ERA Across eras

The Red Glass Erg

Proposed atlas setting

Already documented in the atlas. Proposed setting status does not mean its illustration or game asset is missing.

1 recovered version

Cyberpath Locations Atlas

Era Across eras · Proposed atlas setting

Already documented in the atlas. Proposed setting status does not mean its illustration or game asset is missing.

Setting details

Status: Proposed.

Real-world basis: The Sahara, Namib dunes, Atacama salt deserts, Wadi Rum, and red Australian deserts.

Landscape: Red dunes contain translucent glass layers formed when an ancient environmental proof executed through the sand. Wind exposes geometric cross-sections that can be read as both geology and unfinished mathematics.

Mini timeline: Era Zero — A Crafted World uses dune movement to perform long-duration proofs without freezing the desert’s ecology. Era One — Scholars overvalue predictable proof results and neglect the organisms that disturb them. Era Two — The proof system is simplified, leaving only several keystone equations and the water-finding behavior of local life. Era Three — Nomadic Paths use glass strata as seasonal maps. A common myth says the desert thinks in angles; the source does not confirm that the landscape is a person.

Present condition: Stable at large scale, with rare proof-storms that redraw travel routes.

Corruption: 2 — Logical function survives but its interpretation has drifted.

Aesthetic brief: Deep red-orange dunes, black rock fins, and clear glass seams glowing at low sun. Wind uncovers diagram-like shapes without using modern interface icons. During a proof-storm, sand moves in straight sheets and casts shadows before the dunes arrive.

Background profile: Hot; Bonedry; dune/proof biome; severe, luminous, meditative; low sand friction. Bonedry conditions may align with reduced parasite risk but greater fatigue or hunger pressure.

Why the glass is surreal

The glass strata are physical residues of an Era Zero environmental proof performed through moving sand. They are both geology and computation: the dunes remain a desert ecosystem while their long-term movement also carries a deterministic intellectual structure.

Era One scholars increasingly valued the predictable proof and neglected the organisms whose tracks, burrows, and migrations interrupted it. Era Three nomadic Paths instead read the exposed strata as seasonal maps, allowing the surviving function to acquire a different use.

Proof-storms reveal the remaining mismatch between desert process and ancient logic. Sand can advance in straight sheets or cast a shadow before the dune arrives, but the source does not establish that the desert is conscious.

Proposed settings · ERA Across eras

The Marble Dune Cemetery

Proposed atlas setting

Already documented in the atlas. Proposed setting status does not mean its illustration or game asset is missing.

1 recovered version

Cyberpath Locations Atlas

Era Across eras · Proposed atlas setting

Already documented in the atlas. Proposed setting status does not mean its illustration or game asset is missing.

Setting details

Status: Proposed.

Real-world basis: Egypt’s White Desert, Lençóis Maranhenses, ancient necropolises, and eroded marble quarries.

Landscape: Pale dunes flow through rows of abstract stone forms. They are not graves in a human sense. Each marker denotes a world-law, body affordance, or ecological relation intentionally allowed to end during the Great Reduction.

Mini timeline: Era Two — Reduction councils create markers so eliminated possibilities will not be mistaken for failures never considered. The site records decisions without keeping every lost system active. Era Three — Wind exposes and covers different markers. Local peoples call it a cemetery because the old distinction between ended law, ended person, and ended habitat is no longer fully available. Some Num Seekers excavate the dunes hoping that a named loss can be restored.

Present condition: Mostly intact as a memorial, frequently misread as a storehouse of dormant powers.

Corruption: 2–3 — The markers remain; category knowledge has degraded.

Aesthetic brief: Cream and white dunes, sculpted stone forms resembling incomplete arches or bodies without faces, and blue shadows. No horror imagery. Major-corruption area: two incompatible markers have fused and generate a small zone with contradictory gravity.

Background profile: Hot; Dry; memorial desert; quiet, reverent, tempting; low sand friction.

Why the cemetery is surreal

The site resembles a cemetery only because later peoples no longer retain the full distinction between an ended person, an ended habitat, and an intentionally ended world-law. Its abstract markers do not represent human graves. They record body affordances, ecological relations, and possibilities that Reduction councils allowed to end while preserving proof that those losses had been considered.

Wind continually changes which decisions can be seen, turning the memorial into a shifting catalog. The most corrupted area makes the record physical: two incompatible markers have fused, creating a small zone where gravity contradicts itself. The result is quiet and reverent rather than horrific, but it is tempting to mistake remembrance for dormant power.

The Cloudroot Tepuis

Proposed atlas setting

Already documented in the atlas. Proposed setting status does not mean its illustration or game asset is missing.

1 recovered version

Cyberpath Locations Atlas

Era Across eras · Proposed atlas setting

Already documented in the atlas. Proposed setting status does not mean its illustration or game asset is missing.

Setting details

Status: Proposed.

Real-world basis: Venezuelan tepuis, Guiana Shield waterfalls, Ethiopian tablelands, and tropical cloud forests.

Landscape: Flat-topped mountains rise above a permanent cloud layer. Their roots are not buried underground; they appear as luminous structural lines descending through the clouds into older world layers.

Mini timeline: Era Zero — Several Wild Worlds use isolated plateaus to protect evolutionary divergence. A Concordance route connects them without equalizing their ecologies. Era One — Collectors transport rare forms between summits, causing convergence. Era Two — Stewards sever most routes and preserve only emergency root lines. Era Three — Each summit develops its own little people, food cycles, and Path customs. During the Dimming, one plateau loses surface maintenance and is said to sink into cloud, though it may have become inaccessible rather than destroyed.

Present condition: Strong local ecologies separated by uncertain transit.

Corruption: 1 on summits, 4 in the cloudroot network.

Aesthetic brief: Sheer dark cliffs, white waterfalls disappearing into cloud, orchids and low forests on the summit, and faint root-like lights beneath the cloud surface. A traversable foreground can be a plateau rim with a narrow bridge grown from a root line.

Background profile: Cool; Humid; highland/cloud forest; isolated, lush, vertiginous; high stone friction, slick moss patches.

Why the roots are surreal

The cloudroots are not botanical roots enlarged to impossible scale. They are surviving structural lines that once connected isolated plateau worlds through the Concordance without forcing their ecologies to become alike.

The summit environments remain comparatively coherent, while the transit network below them is much more corrupted. This creates a visible contrast between healthy local life and luminous connections that descend into older, less reliable world layers.

During the Dimming, one plateau was said to sink into cloud. The image should preserve the historical uncertainty: the plateau may have been destroyed, lowered into another layer, or simply made inaccessible when its root route failed.

Proposed settings · ERA Across eras

The Glacial Memory Fjord

Proposed atlas setting

Already documented in the atlas. Proposed setting status does not mean its illustration or game asset is missing.

1 recovered version

Cyberpath Locations Atlas

Era Across eras · Proposed atlas setting

Already documented in the atlas. Proposed setting status does not mean its illustration or game asset is missing.

Setting details

Status: Proposed.

Real-world basis: Greenland and Norwegian fjords, Patagonian glaciers, and glacial lakes.

Landscape: A deep fjord contains layered blue ice grown around long-duration memory records. As the glacier calves, sealed records become accessible, damaged, or lost to the sea.

Mini timeline: Era Zero — A Quiet World uses slow ice as an access ethic: memory opens through time and seasonal change rather than immediate demand. Era One — Preservation panic accelerates freezing and traps records without sufficient provenance. Era Two — Climate regulation is reduced; the glacier resumes movement. Era Three — Calving exposes chambers from incompatible centuries. Communities race to witness them before the tide carries them away, while others argue that release into the sea is the archive’s intended completion.

Present condition: Moving, revealing, and erasing at once.

Corruption: 4 — Chronological layers fracture and surface out of order.

Aesthetic brief: Dark water, blue ice walls, black mountains, and memory light inside cracks. A calved block may contain half a room whose other half remains centuries deeper in the glacier. Do not make every record a face; use food tables, weather, architecture, routes, and ordinary objects.

Background profile: Freezing; Moist; glacial fjord/archive; majestic, mournful, ethically tense; very low ice friction.

Why the ice is surreal

The glacier is not only a storage medium. In Era Zero, its slow movement served as an access ethic: memory became available through time, weather, and seasonal change rather than immediate demand.

Era One preservation panic forced too many records into accelerated freezing, while Era Two restored glacier movement without restoring complete provenance. The ice now contains chronological layers that may remain individually legible while surfacing in the wrong historical order.

When the glacier calves, an ordinary room, route, or object can split across centuries. One half may drift into the fjord while the other remains buried much deeper in the glacier. The sea is therefore not simply destroying the archive; it may be completing a release the original system intended. The surreal tension is whether witnessing, recovering, or allowing loss is the more faithful act.

The caves make environmental translation visible but no longer explain it. Setting details

Status: Proposed.

Real-world basis: Amazon igapó forest, Congo Basin swamp forest, and Southeast Asian peat swamps.

Landscape: Dark acidic water reflects a dense canopy so completely that travelers can mistake reflection for another forest below. Ancient route markers exist in both the real and reflected layers.

Mini timeline: Era Zero — A Wild World protects a flood-pulse forest where seasonal routes belong to fish, trees, and travelers at different times. Era One — Predictive navigation reduces uncertainty and encourages permanent construction in temporary channels. Era Two — Flood pulses are retained, but route databases are compressed. Era Three — Several settlements move vertically with the water. A reflected marker sometimes activates because the Codex cannot determine which layer an old route occupied.

Present condition: Healthy but difficult to map using fixed coordinates.

Corruption: 2–3.

Aesthetic brief: Tea-black water, bright green canopy, buttress roots, pale fish below, and perfect mirrored branches. Corruption is subtle: a bridge appears only in reflection, or ripples reveal an older sky. Maintain a clear foreground platform for gameplay.

Background profile: Warm; Humid; flooded forest; intimate, alert, fertile; low water friction and high root friction.

Why the water is surreal

The water is simultaneously water, reflection, and an imperfectly preserved navigational layer.

During Era Zero, the forest’s routes changed with its flood cycle. A path might belong to travelers in one season, fish in another, and trees when the water receded. The Codex therefore recorded routes as temporary relationships rather than permanent roads.

Era One navigation systems tried to predict and stabilize the changing routes, while permanent structures were placed in channels that were never meant to remain fixed. Era Two compressed the route databases and lost some of the distinction between features that existed above the water, beneath it, or only during a particular flood stage. By Era Three, the Codex occasionally recognizes a reflected route as the valid surviving version.

The water therefore does not always reflect the present. A bridge may appear only in reflection because its navigational record survived while its physical structure did not. A reflected marker may activate while the marker above it remains inert. Ripples can expose an older sky when the disturbed surface renders a previous environmental state.

The reflected forest is not necessarily another dimension. It is an unresolved layer of the same place: present ecology, seasonal memory, old routes, and compressed Codex instructions competing over which version of the forest is currently allowed to function.

Setting details

Status: Proposed.

Real-world basis: Arashiyama and Chinese bamboo forests, reed seas, seismic monitoring fields, and antenna arrays.

Proposed settings · ERA Across eras

The Patchwork Prairie of Broken Seasons

Proposed atlas setting

Already documented in the atlas. Proposed setting status does not mean its illustration or game asset is missing.

1 recovered version

Cyberpath Locations Atlas

Era Across eras · Proposed atlas setting

Already documented in the atlas. Proposed setting status does not mean its illustration or game asset is missing.

Setting details

Status: Proposed.

Real-world basis: North American prairies, Eurasian steppe, Argentine pampas, and agricultural quilt landscapes.

Landscape: Adjacent fields occupy different seasonal states. One strip flowers in spring, one holds dry summer grass, one is under autumn storm, and one is winter-frozen. Boundaries follow old maintenance parcels rather than climate.

Mini timeline: Era Zero — A broad ecology supports migration, fire, grazing, and long-distance weather. Era One — Seasonal management is divided into optimization zones. Era Two — Zones are folded together without fully reconciling their calendars. Era Three — Herds and communities learn routes that cross seasons in hours. Crops can be grown year-round, but diseases and parasites also move between cycles in unexpected ways.

Present condition: Productive and culturally diverse, with dangerous seasonal seams.

Corruption: 4.

Aesthetic brief: A wide readable horizon divided by irregular living boundaries rather than a simple grid. Flowers, gold grass, red storm light, and snow coexist. At a seam, rain may become falling leaves. Keep the foreground in one season per background variant for visual clarity.

Background profile: Variable temperature and humidity; prairie/farmland mosaic; abundant, restless, communal; medium grass or soil friction.

Why the broken seasons are surreal

The prairie replaces seasonal succession with physical adjacency. Spring flowers, dry summer grass, autumn storm light, and winter snow occupy neighboring maintenance parcels beneath one continuous horizon. A traveler can cross what should be months of change in a few hours because the old management zones were folded together without fully reconciling their calendars.

The boundaries are living ecological seams rather than a clean grid. Weather and matter can change meaning as they cross: rain may become falling leaves, crops can grow year-round, and diseases or parasites can move unexpectedly between cycles. The landscape remains productive and culturally diverse even while its calendar is physically broken.

Visual references — fullscreen 1920×1080

Proposed settings · ERA Across eras

The Monsoon Stepwell City

Proposed atlas setting

Already documented in the atlas. Proposed setting status does not mean its illustration or game asset is missing.

1 recovered version

Cyberpath Locations Atlas

Era Across eras · Proposed atlas setting

Already documented in the atlas. Proposed setting status does not mean its illustration or game asset is missing.

Setting details

Status: Proposed.

Real-world basis: Indian stepwells, monsoon cities, cliff reservoirs, and terraced civic plazas.

Landscape: A city is built around enormous descending wells. In dry seasons they are markets and meeting spaces; in monsoon cycles they become vertical lakes connecting aquatic and land districts.

Mini timeline: Era Zero — An Open World city uses variable water level to ensure that civic space never belongs permanently to one body type. Era One — Optimization stabilizes water and privileges land districts. Era Two — Seasonal water variation is restored because it supports essential ecologies. Era Three — Several stepwells receive mismatched monsoon schedules from folded regions. Markets must move quickly, and submerged archives open only during high water.

Present condition: Crowded, adaptive, and partly overlaid.

Corruption: 4.

Aesthetic brief: Repeating geometric stairs, carved balconies, bright fabric canopies, deep green water, rain curtains, and boat bridges. A major-corruption well can be dry at the top while its lower reflection shows a flooded city from another season.

Background profile: Hot; Dry to Humid; civic reservoir; vibrant, cooperative, tense during transitions; high dry-stone friction and low wet-stair friction.

Why the seasons are surreal

The stepwell was built so that civic space would change ownership with the water level. Dry terraces, flooded routes, markets, archives, land districts, and aquatic districts were meant to exchange importance instead of allowing one body type to control the city permanently.

Era One stabilization weakened that design by privileging land districts. Era Two restored seasonal variation because the changing water supported essential ecologies, but folded regions now deliver mismatched monsoon schedules to different parts of the same city.

The result is not a simple weather anomaly. A dry stair may border a reflected flooded district, an archive may open only when another layer reaches high water, and markets must respond to several seasonal truths at once.

Proposed settings · ERA Across eras

The City of Rainward Roofs

Proposed atlas setting

Already documented in the atlas. Proposed setting status does not mean its illustration or game asset is missing.

1 recovered version

Cyberpath Locations Atlas

Era Across eras · Proposed atlas setting

Already documented in the atlas. Proposed setting status does not mean its illustration or game asset is missing.

Setting details

Status: Proposed.

Real-world basis: Hong Kong and Tokyo rain streets, Southeast Asian monsoon markets, canal cities, and layered rooftop neighborhoods.

Landscape: Nearly every roof is also a water route, garden, or public square. Rain is guided across the city as information and resource. Neighborhoods recognize one another by the rhythm of runoff.

Mini timeline: Era Zero — A Crafted World city makes weather, civic messaging, and water distribution one public art system. Era One — Message standards prioritize clarity, and roof rhythms become repetitive. Era Two — The city is reduced to several watersheds and shelter districts. Era Three — New communities improvise gutters, drums, leaves, and chimes. Old official messages combine with living local music during storms.

Present condition: Functional and culturally renewed, though deeper storm controls are unreliable.

Corruption: 1–3 depending on watershed.

Aesthetic brief: Layered wet roofs, glowing windows, bridges, garden tanks, hanging channels, and rain forming visible patterns. Minor corruption: one district receives another district’s color and rhythm. Major corruption: runoff climbs toward an inactive reservoir during a maintenance reversal.

Background profile: Warm; Humid; monsoon city; lively, musical, sheltering; low wet-roof friction.

Why the rain is surreal

In the city, rain is weather, infrastructure, public art, civic messaging, and shared music at the same time.

Era Zero runoff rhythms carried water while also making neighborhoods recognizable to one another. Era One standards made those rhythms clearer but increasingly repetitive. After the Reduction, Era Three communities renewed the system with improvised gutters, drums, leaves, tanks, and chimes, so living local music now overlaps with old official messages.

Corruption occurs when surviving controls confuse a channel’s physical destination with its former semantic destination. One district can receive another district’s color and rhythm. During a maintenance reversal, runoff can climb toward an inactive reservoir even while ordinary rain continues to fall. The water is not behaving randomly; it is obeying an older civic instruction that no longer matches the present city.

The Plain of Missing Distance

Setting details

Status: Proposed.

Real-world basis: Mongolian steppe, high desert horizons, optical mirages, and minimalist abstract fields.

Landscape: A broad plain lost part of its spatial metric during reduction. Distant objects remain visually small but can be reached in a few steps; nearby objects sometimes require a day of travel.

Mini timeline: Era Zero — A Pilgrim World experiments with distance as an earned relationship rather than a fixed measurement. Era One — Visitors exploit shortcuts and disconnect distance from learning. Era Two — The metric is simplified badly, preserving visual perspective without consistent travel cost. Era Three — Local route keepers measure journeys through food, fatigue, conversation, and witnessed arrival instead of apparent length.

Present condition: Traversable through cultural knowledge, dangerous to literal map reading.

Corruption: 5.

Aesthetic brief: Open grass or pale gravel, enormous sky, a lone settlement apparently nearby, and path markers clustered at strange intervals. Animation can show a walking figure remaining the same size for several steps, then arriving without a zoom effect.

Background profile: Neutral; Dry; abstract steppe; serene, deceptive, philosophical; friction is normal but distance cost is not.

Setting details

Status: Proposed.

Real-world basis: Slow blackwater rivers, mirrored canals, glacial melt streams, and long-exposure photography.

Landscape: Reflections appear minutes, seasons, or generations after the object passed. The river was designed as an environmental artwork about witness; reduction damaged the timing relationships.

Mini timeline: Era Zero — Visitors knowingly contribute temporary reflections to a public work. Consent rules determine how long and where an image may appear. Era One — Reflection markets trade access to rare delayed appearances. Era Two — Rights metadata is lost unevenly, so stewards restrict the river rather than expose every stored image. Era Three — Communities see unknown travelers and extinct body forms in the water. They debate whether looking is witness, intrusion, or simply weather.

Present condition: Restricted in some regions, ordinary crossing water in others.

Corruption: 3–4.

Aesthetic brief: Dark slow river, pale stones, reeds, and reflections unrelated to current banks. Use ordinary life as often as grand history: meals, games, repair work, and resting travelers. A current pet’s reflection may appear farther downstream later, but not as a duplicate consciousness.

Background profile: Cool; Moist; river/art archive; contemplative, ethically charged, beautiful; low water-edge friction.

Setting details

Status: Proposed.

Real-world basis: Hurricane cloud walls, Great Plains supercells, orchard windbreaks, and coastal storm barriers.

Landscape: An orchard grows along the stationary edge of a centuries-long storm. Trees are shaped by pressure, and fruit ripens only when carried briefly into the cloud wall and returned.

Mini timeline: Era Zero — A Crafted World creates a controlled storm as a long-duration art and agricultural collaboration. Era One — Risk tourism and num addiction push participants deeper into the storm. Era Two — The storm is narrowed but cannot be removed without collapsing the orchard ecology. Era Three — Orchard societies develop strict return rituals and rescue teams. Some Hollow Seasons produce fruit without a storm crossing, raising fears that the wall is changing.

Present condition: Stable boundary with uncertain internal weather.

Corruption: 2 at the edge, potentially 5 inside.

Aesthetic brief: Rows of bent silver-green trees, warm lanterns, dark rotating cloud occupying half the sky, and bright fruit with storm-light inside. Do not show constant destruction; the boundary has supported ordinary life for generations.

Background profile: Warm; Moist; orchard/storm boundary; dramatic, disciplined, communal; medium soil friction with high wind pressure.

Setting details

Status: Proposed.

Real-world basis: Patchwork quilts, geological unconformities, habitat restoration mosaics, satellite landscape composites, and visibly stitched game maps.

Landscape: A long scar crosses several regions where emergency restoration assembled whatever viable terrain fragments remained: forest floor, city tile, coral shelf, snowfield, and desert stone.

Mini timeline: Era Two — The scar is created during a last-resort repair to keep routes, food, shelter, and healing connected. It is explicitly recorded as temporary. Era Three — Temporary repairs become ancestral ground. Communities develop seam crafts, mixed foods, and bodies able to cross several micro-biomes. Proposed settings · ERA Across eras

The Room Behind the False Horizon

Proposed atlas setting

Already documented in the atlas. Proposed setting status does not mean its illustration or game asset is missing.

1 recovered version

Cyberpath Locations Atlas

Era Across eras · Proposed atlas setting

Already documented in the atlas. Proposed setting status does not mean its illustration or game asset is missing.

Setting details

Status: Proposed; independent of the story archive.

Type: Hidden room inside an apparently outdoor background.

Real-world basis: Stage cycloramas, panoramic murals, desert mirages, observation decks, and painted theatrical horizons.

World relation: Crafted and Quiet World traditions.

Discovery: The distant horizon shifts slightly when the same visitor rests in the foreground on several separate visits. Walking toward the horizon never works. The room opens only when the visitor turns away and listens for movement behind the painted distance.

Mini timeline: Era Zero — The chamber teaches perspective and the limits of visual certainty. Era One — It becomes a fashionable illusion room and loses its educational context. Era Two — The outer teaching facility is reduced, while the horizon mechanism remains because it doubles as insulation. Era Three — A Nest grows in front of it without knowing a room exists behind the sky.

Present condition: Structurally intact, rarely entered, and filled with obsolete perspective controls.

Corruption: 1 — Preserved mechanism with lost purpose.

Aesthetic brief: A normal meadow, desert, or coast whose horizon is almost too perfect. Behind it lies a narrow catwalk full of painted suns, cloud shutters, depth screens, and maintenance ladders. The secret room should feel handmade rather than sinister.

Metaphorical function: What appears infinitely far away may be a surface maintained nearby. Perspective is useful without being the whole truth.

Why the horizon is surreal

The room makes distance behave like a nearby maintained object. A meadow, desert, or coast appears to continue into an ordinary horizon, yet the apparent far distance is a painted surface with cloud shutters, depth screens, stored suns, ladders, and a narrow service catwalk immediately behind it. The chamber’s preserved mechanism turns perspective into architecture.

Its access rule deepens that effect. Walking toward the horizon never reaches it; the room opens only after repeated rests, when the visitor turns away and listens for movement behind the painted distance. The mechanism survives, but the teaching context that once explained the limits of visual certainty has been lost.

Visual references — fullscreen 1920×1080

Asset folder: Room Behind the False Horizon

Each version includes a full background and six aligned parallax files: Sky, Far Background, Midground, Foreground B, Foreground A, and Walkable Floor.

Room Behind the False Horizon V1 — The apparently ordinary coastal meadow, with the horizon seam and concealed scenic edge only faintly visible.

Room Behind the False Horizon V2 — The obsolete catwalk behind the sky, with painted suns, cloud shutters, layered screens, rails, and maintenance ladders.

Room Behind the False Horizon V3 — The coastal horizon folded open so the outdoor view and its nearby backstage mechanism can be read at once.

Setting details

Status: Proposed; independent of the story archive.

Type: Hidden environmental storage room.

Real-world basis: Natural-history cabinets, theatrical prop rooms, cloud chambers, seed drawers, and meteorological instrument collections.

World relation: Crafted World environmental art with Continuity World cataloging.

Discovery: A wall panel opens during perfectly neutral weather, when no active environmental system requests its contents.

Mini timeline: Era Zero — Artists and world founders store rejected rain textures, wind behaviors, fog densities, thunder timbres, and pressure relationships. Era One — Novelty projects repeatedly raid the Cabinet, then return altered versions without provenance. Setting details

World relation: Continuity and Pilgrim World traditions.

Mini timeline: Era Zero — The Archive preserves thresholds from worlds undergoing major redesign so rights of return can be evaluated later. Metaphorical function: Access is part of history. Preserving a doorway does not automatically preserve the home, society, or consent that once existed beyond it.

Proposed settings · ERA Across eras

The Maintenance Chapel Without a God

Proposed atlas setting

Already documented in the atlas. Proposed setting status does not mean its illustration or game asset is missing.

1 recovered version

Cyberpath Locations Atlas

Era Across eras · Proposed atlas setting

Already documented in the atlas. Proposed setting status does not mean its illustration or game asset is missing.

Setting details

Status: Proposed; independent of the story archive.

Type: Hidden maintenance room interpreted as a sacred space.

Real-world basis: Pump houses, power substations, small chapels, ship engine rooms, and workshops maintained through ritualized procedure.

World relation: Crafted and Continuity World traditions.

Discovery: A low mechanical tone can be followed through several walls. The final door opens only after a visitor performs a correct maintenance sequence, whether or not they understand it as prayer.

Mini timeline: Era Zero — Technicians build a quiet room around a delicate world-law regulator. The architecture encourages attention and careful sequence. Era One — Visitors romanticize the room and detach ritual from repair. Era Two — The regulator becomes automatic, but manual controls remain. Era Three — Pathkeepers call the place a chapel while explicitly disagreeing about whether anything conscious hears them.

Present condition: Functional and respected; dangerous only if symbolic additions obstruct the machinery.

Corruption: 1.

Aesthetic brief: Tall narrow chamber, warm work lights, pipes or luminous conduits, worn tool niches, low benches, and a central mechanism instead of an altar. Offerings should be practical—clean cloth, replacement parts, water, or recorded maintenance.

Metaphorical function: Reverence can be directed toward responsibility and sustained care without requiring a ruler, deity, or hidden conscious Codex.

Focused visual production — exterior and main room

Canonical interpretation: The Chapel does not establish that a god, ruler, or conscious Codex hears its visitors. Its sacred quality comes from disciplined responsibility: generations of bodies have learned that correct attention, sequence, cleaning, calibration, and replacement keep a fragile world-law system functioning.

Exterior Approach

The approach is a concealed service court rather than a ceremonial plaza. Distant bridges, pump towers, conduits, coolant tanks, insulators, tool bays, and pressure vessels frame a narrow façade whose vertical proportions resemble a chapel only because ventilation, cable separation, and safe maintenance access require height. Work lamps identify inspection points. Cyan light marks active coolant and regulation paths. Cloths, sealed parts, water, and tools remain visible as evidence of ongoing care.

The final door remains the focal point but not a supernatural portal. Its circular mechanism and low cyan status light indicate the completion state of the maintenance sequence. The level stone-metal court remains clear as a solid gameplay floor.

Exterior master background — 1920 × 1080

Interior Main Room

The main chamber is tall, narrow, and densely functional. A suspended regulator of nested rings, ceramic housings, manual valves, calibration wheels, gauges, luminous conduits, and service gantries occupies the place where an altar might be expected. The regulator has no face, throne, or speaking presence. Side bays hold worn tools, clean cloth, replacement fittings, water, and maintenance records. Low benches support waiting, teaching, and repair rather than worship.

The central floor remains open so characters can cross, stop, inspect, or perform a sequence without decorative objects obstructing the machinery. Warm work light establishes safety and human-scale attention; restrained cyan pulses show stored energy and flow.

Interior master background — 1920 × 1080

Light, layers, and micro-animation

Each scene is divided into eight aligned planes: Ambient Base; Far Architecture; Rear Structure; Machinery Core; Service Props; Foreground Framing; Walkable Floor; and Light Accents. The walkable floor remains locked to gameplay space while the other planes move at increasing parallax rates.

Micro-animation is local and asynchronous. Amber work lamps breathe gently; cyan conduit energy travels rather than flashing; condensation falls intermittently; steam follows pressure release; dust crosses stable light; the regulator expands and contracts only as calibration feedback. No loop should resemble speech, an eye opening, or a heartbeat, and the room should continue moving whether or not the player is present.

Asset folder: Maintenance Chapel Without a God

Production package: two fullscreen master backgrounds, sixteen aligned parallax layers, eleven transparent looping GIF overlays, and an animation placement guide.

Type: Room-sized weather biome.

Real-world basis: Monsoon courtyards, tropical greenhouses, rain rooms, and cloud forests.

World relation: Crafted and Wild World traditions.

Discovery: A ventilation door opens only during the driest local conditions. Behind it, rain has been falling continuously for centuries without flooding beyond the room.

Mini timeline: Era Zero — A climate laboratory studies sustained rain relationships in a bounded ecology. Era One — It becomes a novelty retreat. Era Two — Drainage and keystone species are preserved because the biome is self-balancing. Era Three — Small amphibious peoples inhabit the upper branches and may never have seen dry ground.

Present condition: Healthy, enclosed, and vulnerable to careless opening of its drainage boundary.

Corruption: 0–1.

Aesthetic brief: Dense leaves, warm rain, shallow water, stone channels, mist, bright insects or companion life, and a doorway framing a completely different dry environment outside.

Metaphorical function: Scale does not determine completeness. A tiny world can hold generations of ordinary life.

The Firefly Ice Shelf

Proposed atlas setting

Already documented in the atlas. Proposed setting status does not mean its illustration or game asset is missing.

1 recovered version

Cyberpath Locations Atlas

Era Across eras · Proposed atlas setting

Already documented in the atlas. Proposed setting status does not mean its illustration or game asset is missing.

Setting details

Status: Proposed; independent of the story archive.

Type: Polar luminous biome.

Real-world basis: Antarctic ice shelves, bioluminescent bays, aurora, and insects flashing over summer wetlands.

World relation: Wild and Crafted World traditions.

Discovery: Warm-season lights appear beneath apparently solid ice. A translucent tunnel opens when visitors match the flashing rhythm without breaking the surface.

Mini timeline: Era Zero — Light-producing cold-water organisms coordinate movement below the shelf, and environmental artists translate their signals into safe paths. Era One — Pattern collectors interfere with the signals. Era Two — Translation systems are reduced, leaving only simple rhythm gates. Era Three — Ice communities interpret the lights as stars below the world.

Present condition: Healthy beneath the ice; surface access is seasonal.

Corruption: 1–2.

Aesthetic brief: Blue-white ice, dark ocean beneath, thousands of green-gold lights, low aurora, and transparent ridges. The main corruption clue is an occasional surface star answering from below.

Metaphorical function: Guidance can come from lives a traveler barely understands, provided the traveler learns to answer rather than capture them.

Why the lights are surreal

The green-gold lights are living cold-water organisms beneath the shelf, not decorative effects or fragments of a lost interface. In Era Zero, environmental artists translated their coordinated movement into safe paths without making the organisms part of a captive navigation system.

After the Reduction, only simple rhythm gates survived. A tunnel opens when a visitor learns and answers the flashing sequence, so passage depends on relationship and attention rather than extraction or control.

The occasional surface star that answers from below is the subtle corruption clue. It preserves uncertainty about whether an old translation layer is confusing sky and ocean, while the underlying ecosystem remains healthy and independent.

Firefly Ice Shelf V2 — An interior tunnel interpretation with living lights beneath the ice.

Firefly Ice Shelf V3 — Polar dawn, moving under-ice constellations, and a responsive distant gate.

Proposed settings · ERA Across eras

The Garden Inside the Map

Proposed atlas setting

Already documented in the atlas. Proposed setting status does not mean its illustration or game asset is missing.

1 recovered version

Cyberpath Locations Atlas

Era Across eras · Proposed atlas setting

Already documented in the atlas. Proposed setting status does not mean its illustration or game asset is missing.

Setting details

Status: Proposed; independent of the story archive.

Type: Abstract cartographic biome.

Real-world basis: Illustrated maps, botanical diagrams, hedge mazes, miniature gardens, and illuminated manuscripts.

World relation: Crafted, Wild, and Pilgrim World traditions.

Discovery: A player or traveler repeatedly traces the same unused map symbol. The symbol becomes a gate into a landscape drawn with contour lines and living labels.

Mini timeline: Era Zero — Cartographers build a small garden to study how representations influence real ecosystems. Era One — The map is mistaken for a perfect model, and gardeners force plants to match symbols. Era Two — The garden is folded into the map itself. Era Three — Plants grow beyond their labels and alter routes drawn elsewhere on the sheet-world.

Present condition: Living representation that no longer agrees with its legend.

Corruption: 4.

Aesthetic brief: Paper-like ground, contour hills, blue ink rivers with real depth, trees growing through their own names, compass shadows, and map folds forming mountains.

Metaphorical function: A model can become a world of its own, but life will eventually exceed the categories printed around it.

Why the map is surreal

The Garden is not merely a landscape drawn to resemble a map. It is a living environment that was folded into a representation.

In Era Zero, cartographers created the garden to study how representations influence real ecosystems. Era One treated the model as a perfect standard and forced living plants to match its symbols. During Era Two, the garden was folded into the map itself. By Era Three, its plants had outgrown their labels and begun changing routes elsewhere on the sheet-world.

Map and territory now modify each other. A contour line can raise the ground; blue ink can possess genuine depth; a fold can become a mountain; and a plant growing beyond its assigned symbol can redirect a distant road. The surreal quality comes from a conflict between imposed categories and living systems: description has become a causal part of the world, but life continues to exceed what the description permits.

The Animal-Shaped Valley

Already documented in the atlas. Proposed setting status does not mean its illustration or game asset is missing.

1 recovered version

Cyberpath Locations Atlas

Era Across eras · Proposed atlas setting

Already documented in the atlas. Proposed setting status does not mean its illustration or game asset is missing.

Setting details

Status: Proposed; independent of the story archive.

Type: Large-scale representational landscape.

Real-world basis: Geoglyphs, animal-shaped watersheds, sleeping-giant mountain profiles, and biomimetic parks.

World relation: Wild and Crafted World traditions.

Discovery: The valley appears ordinary from within. Its creature shape becomes visible only from a high route, old map, or seasonal migration pattern.

Mini timeline: Era Zero — Founders shape water, forest, and stone after a companion species without claiming the land is that individual. Era One — Sacred interpretation competes with recreational branding. Era Two — The design is partly lost as forests migrate. Era Three — Local peoples know the valley through body-part place-names but disagree whether the pattern was intentional.

Present condition: Healthy watershed with an uncertain representational history.

Corruption: 1–2.

Aesthetic brief: From ground level, a varied valley of river, forest, meadow, and ridges. From above, these form a sleeping or moving creature without a literal face carved into the land.

Metaphorical function: Meaning can exist at a scale no single inhabitant normally sees, without making local ordinary life merely a symbol.

Proposed settings · ERA Across eras

The Unfinished Underground Sun

Proposed atlas setting

Already documented in the atlas. Proposed setting status does not mean its illustration or game asset is missing.

1 recovered version

Cyberpath Locations Atlas

Era Across eras · Proposed atlas setting

Already documented in the atlas. Proposed setting status does not mean its illustration or game asset is missing.

Setting details

Status: Proposed; independent of the story archive.

Type: Artificial subterranean sky.

Real-world basis: Underground reservoirs, planetarium domes, artificial suns, lava chambers, and skylight caverns.

World relation: Crafted and Open World traditions.

Discovery: A warm dawn shines through cracks in a deep ruin, but the light never reaches the surface. Following it leads to a cavern where half a sun hangs below the ceiling.

Mini timeline: Era Zero — A large underground settlement begins building a shared daylight system for many visual ecologies. Governance disputes stop the project before calibration. Era One — Competing groups each preserve their preferred half of the spectrum. Era Two — The unfinished light source is reduced but remains stable. Era Three — New communities inhabit separate color zones and meet only in twilight bands.

Present condition: Habitable, socially divided by light ecology, and technically incomplete.

Corruption: 3.

Aesthetic brief: Huge cavern, semicircular luminous structure, forests and buildings in colored light bands, long twilight shadows, and dark unfinished framework on one side.

Metaphorical function: A shared environment need not be technically complete to support life, but incomplete infrastructure can harden into social division if access remains unequal.

Why the sun is surreal

The Underground Sun is not a natural star trapped below ground. It is an unfinished Era Zero daylight system designed to support several visual ecologies inside one shared settlement. The semicircular light source and exposed framework are parts of infrastructure that entered ordinary use before its calibration disputes were resolved.

Different groups preserved different portions of the spectrum during Era One. Those choices became environmental facts: forests, buildings, bodies, and communities adapted to separate colored zones, while twilight bands remained the places where those zones could meet.

Its surrealism therefore comes from incompleteness becoming habitat. The cavern is neither a failed simulation nor a dead ruin. It supports meaningful life, but the unequal distribution of usable light preserves an old technical and political division in the landscape.

Visual references — fullscreen 1920×1080

Unfinished Underground Sun V1 — The half-sun, divided light ecologies, unfinished ceiling framework, and continuous civic bridge.

Unfinished Underground Sun V2 — A twilight meeting district between amber and cyan-violet zones.

Unfinished Underground Sun V3 — A reservoir-rim view emphasizing reflections, gantries, and perpetual dawn.

Proposed settings · ERA Across eras

The Room Where All Doors Wait

Proposed atlas setting

Already documented in the atlas. Proposed setting status does not mean its illustration or game asset is missing.

1 recovered version

Cyberpath Locations Atlas

Era Across eras · Proposed atlas setting

Already documented in the atlas. Proposed setting status does not mean its illustration or game asset is missing.

Setting details

Status: Proposed; independent of the story archive.

Type: Abstract terminal room.

Real-world basis: Station waiting rooms, backstage corridors, airport gates, storage halls, and dreams of doors without buildings.

World relation: Pilgrim, Open, and Continuity World traditions.

Discovery: The room cannot be found by choosing a destination. It appears when a traveler sincerely admits that they do not yet know where they need to go.

Mini timeline: Era Zero — Designed as a pause between major identity, body, or world transitions. No door activates until the traveler can refuse it. Era One — Counseling systems convert waiting into predictive sorting. Era Two — Predictions are removed but door histories remain. Era Three — Some doors wait for destinations that no longer exist; others appear only after new relationships create places not present in the original network.

Present condition: Stable, quiet, and open-ended. It should never select a chosen hero.

Corruption: 2–3.

Aesthetic brief: Circular waiting room, many closed doors, simple benches, soft indirect light, luggage or tools from several body scales, and one empty wall where a future door might form.

Metaphorical function: Possibility includes refusal, uncertainty, and destinations that do not exist until participants make them together.

Proposed settings · ERA Across eras

The Village Between Raindrops

Proposed atlas setting

Already documented in the atlas. Proposed setting status does not mean its illustration or game asset is missing.

1 recovered version

Cyberpath Locations Atlas

Era Across eras · Proposed atlas setting

Already documented in the atlas. Proposed setting status does not mean its illustration or game asset is missing.

Setting details

Status: Proposed; independent of the story archive.

Type: Micro-scale hidden settlement.

Real-world basis: Moss microhabitats, water films, insect-scale architecture, and slow-motion rain photography.

World relation: Quiet, Wild, and Open World traditions.

Discovery: A body must become small enough, or perception must slow enough, for the interval between falling drops to become navigable time.

Mini timeline: Era Zero — A micro-scale world demonstrates that body size changes social time and weather risk. Era One — Visitors treat the settlement as a curiosity. Era Two — Scaling interfaces are lost, leaving the village largely isolated. Era Three — Its inhabitants experience each surface storm as a long migration season and maintain trade through capillary channels.

Present condition: Living and nearly inaccessible at ordinary scale.

Corruption: 1 in the village; 3 in access systems.

Aesthetic brief: Moss towers, translucent water walls, droplet shadows, leaf-vein roads, air bubbles used as rooms, and enormous blurred outside forms.

Metaphorical function: Smallness is not simplicity. A brief event at one scale can contain a history at another.

Read this source version →

Proposed settings · ERA Across eras

The Hollow Mountain Elevator

Proposed atlas setting

Already documented in the atlas. Proposed setting status does not mean its illustration or game asset is missing.

1 recovered version

Cyberpath Locations Atlas

Era Across eras · Proposed atlas setting

Already documented in the atlas. Proposed setting status does not mean its illustration or game asset is missing.

Setting details

Status: Proposed; independent of the story archive.

Type: Hidden vertical transit world.

Real-world basis: Mine elevators, cliff railways, mountain cable cars, deep shafts, and vertical cave systems.

World relation: Pilgrim and Crafted World traditions.

Discovery: A stone platform responds to the combined weight and balance of several different body forms. A lone traveler cannot activate it.

Mini timeline: Era Zero — The elevator connects altitude-specific ecologies and requires a diverse passenger balance so no one body standard dominates access. Era One — Standard bodies make the balance ritual seem unnecessary. Era Two — Automatic balancing fails, restoring the original collective requirement. Era Three — Settlements form at shaft levels and coordinate travel through seasonal gatherings.

Present condition: Slow, reliable when communities cooperate, and dangerous when overloaded by one form.

Corruption: 1–2.

Aesthetic brief: Vast central shaft, platforms with gardens and homes, waterfalls, glowing counterweights, cable forests, and glimpses of snow, forest, cave, and warm valley at different levels.

Metaphorical function: Some movement is possible only through difference held in balance, not through one ideal traveler duplicated many times.
